--- SENTENCE 546 ---
APPENDIX A

### ALGAE SURVEY REPORT

by

Posa A. Skelton

--- SENTENCE 547 ---
### **Introduced Marine Species Survey in Pago Pago Harbor, American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
**

### **ALGAE SURVEY REPORT**

By

Posa A. Skelton International Ocean Institute (Australia) PO Box 1539 Townsville, QLD 4810 AUSTRALIA

### **Introduction**

This report was prepared for the Bernice P. Bishop Museum, as part of the introduced marine species survey in American Samoa conducted in October 2002.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
The report analyzes the results of algal surveys from ten sites on Tutuila Island, American Samoa, to determine their status as invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
Algae are an important component of tropical reefs providing food for many organisms including humans, consolidating loose rubble, providing a niche for animals and plants, and complementing the array of colors that continues to entice visitors.

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
They are also good environmental indicators especially when there is a change in the ecosystem such as increased nutrients or the absence of herbivores and grazers (Hatcher and Larkum 1983).

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
One of the more serious threats to the marine environment is introduced invasive algae.

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
Invasive algae have been documented in most oceans and seas.

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
In the Mediterranean approximately 61 species are considered introduced, 28 in the Atlantic coast of Europe, 21 in New Zealand, and about 20 in Southern Australia (Ribera and Boudouresque 1995).

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
Negative ecological impacts are known for some invasive algal species, for example *Acanthophora spicifera, Avrainvillea amadelpha, Gracilaria salicornia* and *Hypnea musciformis* in Hawai'i (Smith et al. 2002), *Undaria pinnatifida* in New Zealand and Australia (Hay and Luckens 1987, Hay 1990, Sanderson 1990), *Codium fragile* ssp.

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
*tomentosoides* in North America (Carlton and Scanlon 1985), *Sargassum muticum* in Canada and Europe (Critchley et al. 1990), and *Caulerpa taxifolia* in the Mediterranean and South Australia (Meinesz and Hesse 1991).

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
In the tropical seas introduced algae are poorly documented, except for the Hawaiian Islands.

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
This may in part be attributed to the lack of expertise in the Pacific Island countries to accurately identify species and the low priority accorded to introduced species in the past.

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
The smothering and subsequent weakening of the reef structure at Kan'eohe Bay, Hawai'i by the introduced *Kappaphycus striatum* (Smith pers.

--- SENTENCE 560 ---
comm.) is of a grave concern as this alga (and other related *Kappaphycus* species) has been introduced to most Pacific Islands for cultivation (Skelton 1998).

--- SENTENCE 561 ---
The introduction of algae into a new place is either accidental, that is attached to ships hulls, equipment used in the aquatic environment, shells of cultured animals and ship's ballast, or deliberate for animal feed or aquaculture purposes (agar and carrageenan extraction).

--- SENTENCE 562 ---
American Samoa's algal flora was first assessed by William Albert Setchell in the early 1920s (Setchell 1924), and 100 species were compiled.

--- SENTENCE 563 ---
Setchell's collections were limited to shallow intertidal areas, with a few subtidal specimens obtained by dredging.

--- SENTENCE 564 ---
No other major algal work is known from American Samoa since Setchell, but the efforts of some sporadic and itinerant algal collectors passing through the islands have yielded some new additions to the flora of the islands.

--- SENTENCE 565 ---
The recent publication of the South Pacific Reef Plants by Diane and Mark Littler (2003) illustrates 33 algal species from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 566 ---
The Littlers' algal collections are housed at the Smithsonian Institution, Washington.

--- SENTENCE 567 ---
The last two phycologists that visited the islands are Drs Paul Gabrielson and Peter Vroom, their collections are yet to be curated.

--- SENTENCE 568 ---
American Samoan algae are also noted from reef ecologists' reports (Dahl 1971, Birkeland et al. 1987, Hunter et al. 1993, Wilkins in Birkeland et al. 1995).

--- SENTENCE 569 ---
Moreover, American Samoa algae were included in the algal checklist of the Samoan Archipelago by Skelton and South (1999), where 198 taxa were listed.

--- SENTENCE 570 ---
The checklist is currently being updated as past collections housed at the Bishop Museum and the University of California at Berkley Herbarium are examined, together with continuing field surveys, adding new records as well as new species to the flora.

--- SENTENCE 571 ---
A total of 318 species have so far been documented (Skelton unpubl.

--- SENTENCE 572 ---
data) for the Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 573 ---
None of the past studies address the issue of introduced algal species.

--- SENTENCE 574 ---
However, they provide an excellent foundation whereby determination of the status of algal introductions can begin.

--- SENTENCE 575 ---
With continuing monitoring of the coral reef ecosystem coupled with the knowledge gleaned and accumulated from past studies, managers should be able to determine the best course of action to safeguard the integrity of the marine environment of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 576 ---
This report provides the first assessment of introduced marine species for the Archipelago.

## **Materials and Methods**

Surveys were carried out from 13-17 October 2002.

--- SENTENCE 577 ---
Algae were collected by snorkeling or by using Scuba gear, wading or shore-collecting, often with the assistance of a diving knife.

--- SENTENCE 578 ---
Smaller and fragile algae were collected together with the substratum they were growing on.

--- SENTENCE 579 ---
All specimens were treated with 4-percent formalin (10% formaldehyde) in seawater solution, and soaked in plastic bags or plastic vials for two days, before draining and repacking them for shipment.

--- SENTENCE 580 ---
Ten sites were surveyed (Table 1), although two smaller collections were made at Leone and Amalau.

--- SENTENCE 581 ---
Details of collection sites and dates.

--- SENTENCE 582 ---
| Station | Depth   | Date       |                            | GPS       |            |

--- SENTENCE 583 ---
| (m)     | Collected  | Locale                     | Lat.

--- SENTENCE 584 ---
|
| 1       | 3-24    | 14 Oct. 02

--- SENTENCE 585 ---
| West Fagatele Bay          | 14°21.96' | 170°45.85' |
| 2       | 5-26    | 14 Oct. 02

--- SENTENCE 586 ---
| East Fagatele Bay          | 14°21.95' | 170°45.77' |
| 3       | +0.5-18 | 13 Oct. 02 | Utulei,

--- SENTENCE 587 ---
Hbr      | 14°17.02' | 170°40.67' |
| 4       | +0.5-8  | 15 Oct. 02 | Main Dock, Pago Pago

--- SENTENCE 588 ---
Hbr   | 14°16.59' | 170°41.26' |
| 5       | 2-14    | 15 Oct. 02 | Dry Dock, Pago Pago

--- SENTENCE 589 ---
Hbr    | 14°16.32' | 170°41.54' |
| 6       | 0-24    | 17 Oct. 02 | Onesosopo, Pago Pago

--- SENTENCE 590 ---
Hbr   | 14°17.18' | 170°39.89' |
| 7       | 2-23    | 17 Oct. 02 | Aua, Pago Pago

--- SENTENCE 591 ---
Hbr         | 14°16.70' | 170°40.16' |
| 8       | 2-22    | 17 Oct. 02 | Leloaloa, Pago Pago

--- SENTENCE 592 ---
Hbr    | 14°16.22' | 170°40.61' |
| 8       | 4-28    | 16 Oct. 02 | Vatia Bay, National e Park | 14°14.79' | 170°40.10' |
| 10      | 4-21    | 16 Oct. 02 | Fagasa Bay, National Park  | 14°17.01' | 170°43.36' |

Standard phycological methods were followed (see Tsuda and Abbott 1985).

--- SENTENCE 593 ---
Macro-algae (large seaweeds of size 5 cm or more) were identified and pressed in a standard plant-presser, and if necessary parts of the plant were removed for anatomical assessment to confirm the species identification.

--- SENTENCE 594 ---
Macro-algal epiphytes were removed and identified.

--- SENTENCE 595 ---
Algae smaller than 5 cm were identified using a Nikon SMZ645 dissecting microscope and an Olympus CX31 compound microscope.

--- SENTENCE 596 ---
Smaller specimens were mounted on slides in a 50% Karo solution (corn syrup in water with a few thymol crystals).

--- SENTENCE 597 ---
Photographs were taken of some of the algae using a Nikon Coolpix 990 digital camera.

--- SENTENCE 598 ---
Each specimen analyzed was designated a field number, which was entered into a log-book.

--- SENTENCE 599 ---
The field number began with the letters AS (for American Samoa) followed by a consequent number.

--- SENTENCE 600 ---
These were later computed into a Microsoft Excel Sheet for further documentation and analysis.

--- SENTENCE 601 ---
Voucher specimens are deposited in the Herbarium Pacificum of Bishop Museum (Honolulu), with some duplicate specimens at the South Pacific Regional Herbarium at the Marine Studies Programme, the University of the South Pacific, Fiji.

### **Results**

A total of 313 specimens were analyzed comprising 139 taxa: 33 Chlorophyta, 9 Phaeophyta, 84 Rhodophyta and 13 Cyanophyta.

--- SENTENCE 602 ---
*Station Location and Site Description*

Stations 1 and 2: West and East Fagatele Bay (14<sup>o</sup> 21.96'S, 170<sup>o</sup> 45.85')

Stations 1 and 2 are located within the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary, which is a moderately exposed bay with relatively steep cliffs and an undulating coastline.

--- SENTENCE 603 ---
A few pockets of flat intertidal areas exist joining the steep cliffs to a gradual sloping fringing reef.

--- SENTENCE 604 ---
The two stations are treated here as one algal ecological habitat due to the continuous fringing reef and their close proximity to each other.

--- SENTENCE 605 ---
The stations were dominated by articulate and non-articulate or crustose coralline algae, as well as small turf algal communities.

--- SENTENCE 606 ---
A buoy is located in the middle of the Bay, and algae attached to it were collected.

--- SENTENCE 607 ---
Algae were also collected from the intertidal area as well as the splash zone.

--- SENTENCE 608 ---
The two sites are not considered high risk sites to introduced species.

#### Station 3: Utulei Pago Pago Harbor (S14<sup>o</sup> 17.04', W170<sup>o</sup> 40.62')

--- SENTENCE 609 ---
The reef flat was surveyed from the shoreline toward the fringing reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 610 ---
Subtidal sites were surveyed by the team.

--- SENTENCE 611 ---
The reef flat frequently has pockets of deeper (3-5 m depth) sandy pits, which were barren at most parts or dominated by soft corals.

--- SENTENCE 612 ---
Debris, rubbish and other structures littered parts of the sandy pits.

--- SENTENCE 613 ---
Digital photos were taken of the site as well as some specimens collected.

--- SENTENCE 614 ---
The current flow and the fact that the site is nearer to the harbor entrance may be a reason why algal communities seem more abundant there.

--- SENTENCE 615 ---
Small red algal turfs (*Gelidiella repens*, *Gelidium* spp.) were common in crevices and on the undersides of limestone rocks and volcanic rocks strewn throughout the site.

--- SENTENCE 616 ---
A few green macro-algae were obvious (*Valonia fastigiata, Dictyosphaeria versluysii, Bryopsis pennata* and *Halimeda gracilis*).

--- SENTENCE 617 ---
*Hypnea pannosa* was found abundant between coral colonies.

--- SENTENCE 618 ---
Two main habitats were distinguished: (i) the shallow reef flat and (ii) deeper sandy pits.

--- SENTENCE 619 ---
Collections were also made from the sea-wall, which consisted mainly of small algal fuzz.

--- SENTENCE 620 ---
This site is considered a high risk to introduced species due to its location.

#### Station 4 and 5: Main (14<sup>o</sup> 16.59, 170<sup>o</sup> 41.26') and Dry (14<sup>o</sup> 16.32', 170<sup>o</sup> 39.89') Docks, respectively - Pago Pago Harbor

Stations 4 and 5 – algal samples were collected from hulls of vessels and pilings.

--- SENTENCE 621 ---
Station 4 (Main Dock) samples were scraped off the hull of an out-of-use landing craft ship with *Oregon, USA* written on the side.

--- SENTENCE 622 ---
At the water-line mark, little turf algae were collected.

--- SENTENCE 623 ---
One of the anchor/stabilizer ropes had a small clump of a black colored alga (*Grateloupia filicina*), mimicking a feather-star.

--- SENTENCE 624 ---
This was collected, with a few plants preserved in alcohol and the rest in formalin

solution.

--- SENTENCE 625 ---
The wharf metal and concrete pilings were also sampled.

--- SENTENCE 626 ---
I collected a number of animals for the group, including an urchin, a shrimp, crabs, a goby and pipe fish and numerous gastropods.

--- SENTENCE 627 ---
Station 5 (Dry Dock), samples were scraped off the hull of *Lien Fan Noi* fishing vessel.

--- SENTENCE 628 ---
The team collected algae, amongst other things, from the shallow subtidal.

--- SENTENCE 629 ---
Both sites are high risk to receiving non-native organisms.

#### Station 6.

--- SENTENCE 630 ---
Onesosopo, Pago Pago Harbor (14<sup>o</sup> 17.18', 170<sup>o</sup> 39.89')

--- SENTENCE 631 ---
This site is located on the opposite side of Utulei on the other side of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 632 ---
A short fringing reef (<100 m) extends seaward before a gradual slope in some parts, whereas vertical walls are common in other parts.

--- SENTENCE 633 ---
The intertidal area was surveyed including the splash-zone near Breaker Point.

--- SENTENCE 634 ---
Some subtidal algal collections were made by the team.

--- SENTENCE 635 ---
Onesosopo is a relatively high risk site for introduced species.

#### Station 7.

--- SENTENCE 636 ---
Aua, Pago Pago Harbor (14<sup>o</sup> 16.70', 170<sup>o</sup> 40.16').

--- SENTENCE 637 ---
The Aua site is on the same harbor side as Onesosopo.

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
The fringing reefs are very much like Onesosopo extending seaward for approximately 100 meters.

--- SENTENCE 639 ---
Some patch reefs are found in this site.

--- SENTENCE 640 ---
Like the previous site, this is considered a high risk site for introduced species.

#### Station 8.

--- SENTENCE 641 ---
Leloaloa Pago Pago Harbor (14<sup>o</sup> 16.22', 170<sup>o</sup> 40.61')

Subtidal sites were surveyed only.

--- SENTENCE 642 ---
The reef contour follows a similar pattern to that of Onesosopo and Aua reefs.

--- SENTENCE 643 ---
A high risk site for receiving introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 644 ---
Vatia Bay, National Marine Park (14<sup>o</sup> 14.79', 170<sup>o</sup> 40.10')

One of the most beautiful places on the island, this predominantly subtidal marine park is fairly inaccessible because of the direct pounding of the waves along the coast.

--- SENTENCE 645 ---
The dominant algal groups were the corallines.

--- SENTENCE 646 ---
The use of scuba equipment is recommended for surveying at this site.

--- SENTENCE 647 ---
Very limited collections were possible along the coast.

--- SENTENCE 648 ---
Some algal specimens were collected by the team from subtidal sites.

--- SENTENCE 649 ---
A small but important collection was made at Amalau Bay, the launching site for the boat.

--- SENTENCE 650 ---
The large volcanic rocks were surveyed and some hardy algal specimens were collected, e.g. *Gelidiella acerosa* and *Sargassum anapense.*

--- SENTENCE 651 ---
This remote site is not considered a high risk of receiving introduced species.

#### Station 10.

--- SENTENCE 652 ---
Fagasa Bay, National Marine Park (14<sup>o</sup> 17.01', 170<sup>o</sup> 43.36')

This site is rivaled by Vatia Bay as one of the picturesque sites of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 653 ---
From an algal perspective this site was very interesting.

--- SENTENCE 654 ---
The diversity of fleshy macro-algae collected from this site was higher than any other sites of the survey.

--- SENTENCE 655 ---
The fringing reefs and slope as well as many crevices and groves provided different habitats, which were occupied by different algae.

--- SENTENCE 656 ---
As like other sites, articulate and non-articulate coralline algae were the dominant group.

--- SENTENCE 657 ---
Some other fleshy algae were seen for the first time including *Turbinaria ornata* and *Chrysymenia kaernbachii*.

--- SENTENCE 658 ---
A low risk site from introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 659 ---
### *Algal Results By Station*

### Stations 1 and 2. (Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary)

--- SENTENCE 660 ---
A total of 47 taxa were found from Fagatele consisting of 3 Cyanophyta, 11 Chlorophyta, 5 Phaeophyta, and 28 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 661 ---
One new addition to the Tutuila Island flora is *Derbesia marina*.

--- SENTENCE 662 ---
This alga is not considered invasive as its distribution is widespread from tropical to coldwater places.

### Station 3. (Utulei)

Fifty six algae were assessed consisting of 5 Cyanophyta, 17 Chlorophyta, 5 Phaeophyta, and 29 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 663 ---
Of all the Pago Pago Harbor sites this is by far the most biologically diverse from an algal perspective.

--- SENTENCE 664 ---
Three new records for the Tutuila Islands were found (*Acetabularia exigua, Codium mammilosum,* and *Hypoglossum anomalum*)*.*

--- SENTENCE 665 ---
All these algae have been recorded from neighboring Western Samoa and Fiji.

### Stations 4 and 5 (Docks)

--- SENTENCE 666 ---
A mere 9 species were collected from the two Docks comprising 1 Cyanophyta, 3 Chlorophyta, and 5 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 667 ---
No Phaeophyta were found.

--- SENTENCE 668 ---
### Station 6 (Onesosopo)

--- SENTENCE 669 ---
A total of 33 algae were recorded, 2 Cyanophyta, 8 Chlorophyta, and 23 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 670 ---
No Phaeophyta were found .

### Station 7 (Aua);

Thirty-four taxa were enumerated comprising of 4 Cyanophyta, 8 Chlorophyta, 1 Phaeophyta, and 21 Rhodophyta.

### Station 8 (Leloaloa)

--- SENTENCE 671 ---
Only 13 taxa were found, comprised of 3 Chlorophyta, and 10 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 672 ---
No cyanophytes or phaeophytes were collected, which may reflect the limited collections made and the season.

--- SENTENCE 673 ---
### Station 9 (Vatia)

--- SENTENCE 674 ---
A total of 31 taxa was recorded, comprised of 3 Cyanophyta, 5 Chlorophyta, 4 Phaeophyta, and 19 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 675 ---
The most unusual find from this site was the collection of a small green alga – *Sporocladopsis erythraea*.

--- SENTENCE 676 ---
This rarely known alga was first recorded from the Red Sea.

--- SENTENCE 677 ---
Its troubled taxonomic history is attributed to Papenfuss (1962) who erroneously synonymized it with *Pilinia* (a Phaeophyta).

--- SENTENCE 678 ---
This alga was found epiphytic on the discoid holdfast of the endemic *Sargassum anapense*.

--- SENTENCE 679 ---
This is the first record of this alga from the Pacific Ocean, but it may well be more common than we think.

--- SENTENCE 680 ---
It may often be overlooked during surveys due to their minute size and cryptic habit.

### Station 10 (Fagasa)

Forty-two taxa were recorded comprising of 4 Cyanophyta, 6 Chlorophyta, 4 Phaeophyta, and 28 Rhodophyta.

--- SENTENCE 681 ---
One new addition to the Samoan flora is *Chrysymenia kaernbachii* a species with its type locality from Papua New Guinea and which has recently been found in Fiji and neighboring Western Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 682 ---
### *Comparison with Previous Surveys*

Of the 318 algae recorded for the Samoa Archipelago, 262 are now known for American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 683 ---
Our surveys found 65 new records for American Samoa (of which approximately 10 are new records for the Archipelago's flora).

--- SENTENCE 684 ---
Setchell (1924) in his treatment of algae from American Samoa compiled 100 species consisting of 13 Cyanophyta, 47 Rhodophyta, 11 Phaeophyta, and 29 Chlorophyta.

--- SENTENCE 685 ---
Setchell, with assistance from three able collectors (Alfred G. Mayor, A. L. Treadwell, and F.A. Potts), collected from 15 different sites within the island of Tutuila plus nearby islets (Aunu'u and Goat).

--- SENTENCE 686 ---
One of the sites that received much attention during Setchell's surveys was Aua Reef including Breaker Point.

--- SENTENCE 687 ---
From this site alone, he compiled 51 species of which four he described as new to science.

--- SENTENCE 688 ---
Our surveys yielded 34 taxa from the Aua Reef site, of which eight were found to be new additions to Setchell's 1924 list.

--- SENTENCE 689 ---
The eight new additions have a pan-tropical distribution and most have been recorded from Western Samoa and as far east as French Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 690 ---
Setchell made a few collections from Fagasa (9 species) and Utulei (4 species – as Utelei), but no collections were made from Vatia, Onesosopo, Leloaloa and Fagatele.

--- SENTENCE 691 ---
Approximately 12 algal taxa were collected by Richard Buggeln and Roy Tsuda from Vatia Bay and Fagasa Bay in 1964 (these are housed at Bishop Museum).

--- SENTENCE 692 ---
Their collections need to be examined, although their tentative identifications include species with a pan-tropical to subtropical distribution.

--- SENTENCE 693 ---
The Fagatele National Marine Sanctuary has been studied over the last fifteen years (Birkeland et al., 1987, 1995) and is considered one of the best monitored marine ecosystems in the Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 694 ---
In the surveys of Birkeland et al. (ibid) they found a very high algal cover (> 75%), comparable to the estimation made during our surveys.

--- SENTENCE 695 ---
The number of algal species reported during their ecological surveys was 39 species in 1987 and 26 in the 1995 survey.

--- SENTENCE 696 ---
This compared to 47 species found during our surveys.

--- SENTENCE 697 ---
This slight increase in species number is attributed primarily on the different habitats that were sampled during our surveys, which include the upper intertidal and the spray-zone, habitats not sampled by Birkeland and team.

### *Nonindigenous and Cryptogenic Species*

As previously stated all of the identified species from our surveys have distributions that are either pan-tropical or limited to the Indo-Pacific region.

--- SENTENCE 698 ---
It is important to note that some marine plant species have spread to as far as American Samoa and no further.

--- SENTENCE 699 ---
For example American Samoa is the easternmost limit for the natural spread of mangroves.

--- SENTENCE 700 ---
Seagrasses (*Halophila ovalis* and *Syringodium isoetifolium*) find their easternmost limit here, being absent from the Cook Islands and then reappearing with a different species in French Polynesia (*Halophila decipens*).

--- SENTENCE 701 ---
This may indicate that the distribution of some marine plants is still continuing eastward.

--- SENTENCE 702 ---
It is highly possible that some of the algae recorded in our surveys have recently been brought in from neighboring countries, particularly Western Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
Two species that could be considered in this category are *Caulerpa serrulata* and *Halymenia durvillei*.

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
These two species are found abundant at the Apia Harbor and were found to be common in Utulei.

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
It is interesting to note that these two algae have an Indo-Pacific distribution but had not been recorded from American Samoa by Setchell, despite the fact that both species are fairly large.

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
Another species of interest is *Codium mamillosum*.

--- SENTENCE 707 ---
Although only a juvenile specimen was collected, it is noted that *Codium* species are notorious in terms of invasiveness.

--- SENTENCE 708 ---
*Codium arenicola* is a species found in Southeast Asia but it appears to have spread as far east as Apia Harbor including Suva Harbor (pers.

--- SENTENCE 709 ---
This alga is yet to be found in Pago Pago Harbor, although with frequent commuting between Apia Harbor and Pago Pago, it may find its way here.

--- SENTENCE 710 ---
Another *Codium* species that is of concern worldwide is *Codium fragilis* ssp.

--- SENTENCE 711 ---
*tomentosoides* with an Atlantic origin has spread to North America, the Mediterranean and New Zealand.

--- SENTENCE 712 ---
*Grateloupia filicina* is an alga with its type locality from Italy was collected from the main Dock.

--- SENTENCE 713 ---
It would be tempting to label this alga as invasive species but it has been recorded from Hawai'i (Abbott 1999) and French Polynesia (Payri and N'Yeurt 1997).

--- SENTENCE 714 ---
There is one alga that has a disjunct distribution, *Sporocladopsis erythraea*.

--- SENTENCE 715 ---
This minute green alga was recorded from the Red Sea by Nasr (1944).

--- SENTENCE 716 ---
There are only two *Sporocladopsis* species known with the second species (*Sporocaldopsis novae-zealandiae*) found in New Zealand and Australia (Millar and Kraft 1994).

--- SENTENCE 717 ---
The occurrence of this alga in our collections was a surprise find, as it was epiphytic on the base of a *Sargassum anapense* an endemic brown alga to Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 718 ---
We consider the presence of *Sporocladopsis erythraea* as native, and it may well be more common than currently reported, but its small size and cryptic habitat may contribute to its being overlooked.

--- SENTENCE 719 ---
### **Discussion**

The algal diversity within the tropical Pacific Ocean to some extent generally follows the pattern seen in other tropical marine organisms (e.g. corals, fishes, gastropods, etc.), with the highest diversity found in the Indo-Malay region.

--- SENTENCE 720 ---
Algae, unlike other tropical marine organisms have their highest species diversity in a few but widely dispersed areas ranging from temperate (South Australia), sub-tropical (southern Japan) to tropical Philippines (Bolton 1994).

--- SENTENCE 721 ---
All of these places contain over 800 species, and their floras continue to receive much attention from scientists.

--- SENTENCE 722 ---
By contrast, the Pacific Islands have had little scientific study of their marine flora.

--- SENTENCE 723 ---
Of the few better known places [Hawai'i (400 spp.), Fiji (500 spp.), French Polynesia, and Samoa (both with over 300 spp.)]

--- SENTENCE 724 ---
many areas and reefs remain to be surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 725 ---
These surveys are important as they provide the baseline information needed when discussing introduced marine species.

--- SENTENCE 726 ---
Fortunately for American Samoa, the algal inventory began in the early 1920s.

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
Although, the flora of the neighboring islands of Western Samoa began much earlier with an inventory carried out by Grunow (1874) followed by Reinbold (1896).

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
It is important to note that the marine flora of the two countries are very similar and therefore should be considered as one (except for the remote atolls of Swains and Rose part of American Samoa).

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
The updated algal list for the Archipelago, compiled by Skelton and South (1999, 2002) provides a checklist against possible recent introductions.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
We must be cautious as we acknowledge that many cryptic species and species found only in deeper waters were probably missed by earlier collectors, and careful analyses need to be made to determine their introduced status.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
For example, the red alga *Chrysymenia kainbachii* was first described from Papua New Guinea, and was collected in our surveys from the Fagasa site.

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
This alga has not been listed in previous algal compilations from the Archipelago and it could easily be considered as an introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
However, this alga has been recorded from Fiji and as far north as Hawai'i.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
Moreover, the site where it was collected (Fagasa) is fairly remote from potential source of introductions such as ports and harbors.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
It is therefore unlikely to be an introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
Utulei was found to be the most diverse site with 56 species recorded and 16 species were new additions to Setchell's list.

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
Fagatele and Fagasa with 47 and 42 species, respectively, were the next sites with high diversity.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
The numbers of new additions from these two sites were 16 species for Fagatele and 15 for Fagasa.

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
The majority of the new additions consist of minute epiphytic algae such as *Herposiphonia secunda, Griffithsia subcylindrica, Dictyopteris repens* and *Hypoglossum anomalum*.

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
Some larger seaweeds are also found from our surveys include *Halymenia durvillei* (*ca* 20 cm tall), *Caulerpa serrulata* (spread to > 10 cm, 5 cm tall) and *Galaxaura filamentosa* (5 cm).

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
*Halymenia durvillei* is an edible seaweed in Samoa (known locally as *Limu mumu* or *Limu aau*).

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
There has been an increased abundance of this seaweed in the Apia Harbor, Western Samoa (pers.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
*Caulerpa serrulata* is also found abundant in the Apia Harbor, especially near the break-wall by the Matautu Wharf (pers.

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
Both these algae were collected from Utulei (by the Harbor entrance).

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
It is reasonable to assume the two algae to have been introduced into the Pago Pago Harbor from neighboring Apia Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
Although more studies including molecular work will need to be undertaken to confirm this.

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
*Galaxaura filamentosa* is a red alga that is often covered in fine silt, thus could have been easily overlooked.

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
It was collected from all of the sites except the docks, thus it is considered part of the native flora.

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
Of the six sites that are considered vulnerable to introduced species (Aua, Docks 1 and 2, Leloaloa, Onesosopo and Utulei), the algal flora was found to be very similar to those from other less vulnerable sites.

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
Only one alga *Grateloupia filicina* was found to be an anomaly in the flora, although it has been reported from Fiji (South and Skelton 2003, in prep.), Hawai'i (Abbott 1999) and from French Polynesia (Payri and N'Yeurt 1997).

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
This alga could be considered a recent introduction as it was only found at the main dock attached to a rope that was anchoring a landing craft.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
This is the first record of this species from the Archipelago.
